DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark
of How Agents Work with Users
Yifei Tao1,4,∗, Xinyu Zhong4,∗, Henry Hengyuan Zhao2,∗, Fanyi Wang4,∗,‡
Tengda Guo4, Wentao Qiu4, Ying Wang3 and Liujian Tang4,†
1Nanyang Technological University, 2National University of Singapore, 3University of Hong Kong, 4Stepfun
Dataset
GitHub
Project Page
Long-term agents must remember not only what is true about a user, but also how a particular agent should work
with that user as their shared history evolves. Existing benchmarks primarily supervise user facts and preferences
or experience reusable across users, leaving this relationship-specific agent memory implicit. Additionally,
most prior works measure the model solely with final-answer QA over long interaction histories, making the
assessment still incomplete and unreliable. To this end, we introduce DyadMem with the proposed new definition
User-conditioned Relational Agent Memory (URAM). DyadMem jointly annotates user-side memory and URAM
along the same multi-session trajectories, resulting in 6 memory categories. To summarize, it includes 3,065
episodes, 50,961 sessions, and 61,210 QA instances, with extensive session-level Capture and Update gold
annotations, query-level Recall support, and two QA settings: Gold-Memory and Full-Pipeline. Across 16
open-weight and 4 proprietary models, Gold-Memory QA is consistently strong, yet Full-Pipeline QA drops
sharply. Such a gap explicitly supports our fine-grained evaluation design. Additionally, several quantitative
results further reveal low Capture recall, incomplete Recall, and unsafe-deletion issues arising from even the
frontier LLMs. We further conduct a rigorous experiment to validate the effectiveness of our URAM and observe
the positive effects for all 20 models. In summary, DyadMem is a dual-domain, full-pipeline memory benchmark
with extensive annotation efforts for advancing the domain’s development.

Figure 1: DyadMem is a dual-domain, full-pipeline memory benchmark. Across multi-session user–agent interactions,
Capture extracts current-session memories, Update reconciles them with the prior bank, Recall selects valid terminal-
bank entries, and QA uses the selected evidence. The evolving bank contains shared episodic memory, user-side memory,
and User-conditioned Relational Agent Memory (URAM); unlike general agent memory that transfers across users,
URAM records how this particular agent should work with this particular user.

arXiv:2610.03020v1  [cs.AI]  2 Oct 2026


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
1
INTRODUCTION
Long-term interaction (Maharana et al., 2024; Wu et al., 2025; Yao et al., 2024) requires more than remember-
ing the past. An agent must retain important facts about the user (Du et al., 2024; Xu et al., 2022; Jiang et al.,
2025b), but it must also learn how its own working relationship with that user has changed (Chao et al., 2026;
Uddin et al., 2026; Wu et al., 2026; Mehri et al., 2026).
Prior work has studied user memory and agent experience largely along separate lines. User-memory
benchmarks model facts, profiles, preferences, and their evolution (Jiang et al., 2025a; Pakhomov et al., 2025;
Uddin et al., 2026; Chao et al., 2026), while experience-based agent memory extracts workflows, environment
knowledge, and failure patterns that can transfer across tasks (Wang et al., 2025; Fang et al., 2026; Wu et al.,
2026). Recent systems further adapt behavior from user corrections and long-term feedback (Mehri et al.,
2026). Across these lines, a complementary memory object remains largely implicit: what should this agent
do differently for this particular user? We call this missing form of memory User-conditioned Relational
Agent Memory (URAM). URAM is not another description of the user: two agents serving the same user may
require different future behavior because their interaction histories differ. This motivates a dual-domain view
of long-term memory that represents both user-side memory and user-conditioned agent-side memory.
How memory is evaluated creates a second gap. Widely used long-term-memory benchmarks commonly
measure success through downstream QA over long interaction histories (Maharana et al., 2024; Wu et al.,
2025; Du et al., 2024). This endpoint is important, but it collapses distinct capabilities: constructing the right
memories, maintaining them as information changes, retrieving complete support, and using that support
in an answer. Recent benchmarks begin to expose selected operations or failure stages (Cheng et al., 2026;
Chen et al., 2026; Hao et al., 2026), but do not jointly supervise this full lifecycle for both user-side and
relationship-specific agent memory. Consequently, a correct answer can mask an incomplete or unsafe
memory bank, while an incorrect answer cannot identify where the pipeline failed. Evaluating relational
memory therefore requires both memory domains to be tracked on the same trajectory and each stage of their
lifecycle to be measured directly.
We therefore introduce DyadMem, a dual-domain, full-pipeline benchmark for long-term memory. DyadMem
follows a dialogue-first construction process: complete conversations are generated and verified before
user-side memory, URAM, and their changes are annotated from the realized interaction. It contains 3,065
episodes, 50,961 sessions, and 61,210 QA instances across 5 major domains and 25 subdomains. As
summarized in Figure 1, session-level Capture and Update annotations and query-level Recall supports
separately evaluate memory construction, updating, and retrieval. Gold-Memory QA measures answer
generation when the annotated supporting memories are supplied directly, whereas Full-Pipeline QA answers
from a memory bank constructed and retrieved by the model itself. Together, the stage-level tasks localize
failures, and the two QA settings quantify their end-to-end consequence.
Across 16 open-weight models and 4 proprietary models, DyadMem exposes failures hidden by final-answer
evaluation. Gold-Memory QA ranges from 78.7% to 96.3%, yet Full-Pipeline QA averages only 47.7% for
open-weight models and 57.4% for proprietary models. Alongside this gap, stage-level results reveal low
Capture recall, incomplete Recall, and unsafe-deletion issues. These observations indicate our benchmark
assessment is comprehensive and holistic. We further conduct experiment to validate the effects of involving
user conditions in agent memory construction. The results show that jointly incorporating user-side memory
and URAM outperforms general experience memory across all 20 LLMs, demonstrating the essential necessity
of our design, which is hardly considered in previous benchmarks.
Our contributions are as follows:
• We formalize relationship-specific agent memory. We introduce URAM to represent how a particular
agent should work with a particular user as their shared history evolves.
• We introduce a large-scale dual-domain memory benchmark. DyadMem jointly annotates user-side
memory, URAM, and shared episodic memory on the same 3,065 multi-session trajectories, with
2


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
dialogue-grounded lifecycle supervision.
• We evaluate the complete memory pipeline. DyadMem separately evaluates Capture, gold-conditioned
standalone Update, Recall, Gold-Memory QA, and Full-Pipeline QA, making failures localizable rather
than visible only through final-answer accuracy.
2
RELATED WORK
What long-term memory represents. LoCoMo (Maharana et al., 2024), LongMemEval (Wu et al., 2025),
and PerLTQA (Du et al., 2024) evaluate long-horizon conversational or personal memory. ConvoMem
(Pakhomov et al., 2025), PersonaMem (Jiang et al., 2025b), Memora (Uddin et al., 2026), and STALE
(Chao et al., 2026) further model changing profiles, preferences, and obsolete information. Their supervised
memory contents nevertheless primarily describe the user. A parallel line extracts reusable task experience:
Agent Workflow Memory (Wang et al., 2025), Memp (Fang et al., 2026), and LongMemEval-V2 (Wu et al.,
2026) capture workflows or dynamic task knowledge, while MemoryAgentBench (Hu et al., 2026) and
Mem2ActBench (Shen et al., 2026) study incremental memory for action. DyadMem differs by representing
both the user and how a particular agent should work with that user on the same interaction trajectory. URAM
is relationship-specific rather than a generic user profile or task-transferable experience.
User-conditioned agent behavior. PrefEval (Zhao et al., 2025) evaluates preference following, MultiSession-
Collab (Mehri et al., 2026) derives actionable reflections from cross-session corrections, and PAHF (Liang
et al., 2026), SAGER (Tao et al., 2026), PersonaAgent (Zhang et al., 2026), and MemToolAgent (Er et al.,
2026) adapt behavior through user-specific memory, policies, prompts, or feedback. These methods establish
the value of adaptation but generally assess the resulting behavior rather than directly supervise evolving
agent-side memory. DyadMem makes this memory explicit: URAM entries are typed, grounded in dialogue
provenance, co-annotated with user-side memory on the same interaction trajectory, and labeled when they
are added, revised, or invalidated. This makes relational adaptation directly inspectable.
Lifecycle-level evaluation of long-term memory. Prior benchmarks expose different parts of the memory
process. LongMemEval (Wu et al., 2025) and PerLTQA (Du et al., 2024) analyze retrieval and answer
construction, AMemGym (Cheng et al., 2026) probes write, read, and utilization failures; HaluMem (Chen
et al., 2026), MemOps (Hao et al., 2026), and Memora (Uddin et al., 2026) provide extraction, lifecycle,
or state-transition supervision; and MemProbe (Ma et al., 2026) audits stored artifacts through user-state
recovery. As summarized in Table 1, DyadMem brings these capabilities together for two coupled memory
domains: per-session gold memory for construction, gold update operations for maintenance, and query-to-
bank supports for retrieval. Gold-Memory QA reads those recall supports as given evidence, and Full-Pipeline
QA composes the three stages, so the two answer settings measure the resulting loss rather than a further
annotation.
Table 1: Comparison with related works. ✓denotes direct support, △related but non-equivalent supervision, and × no
corresponding supervision. Per-session gold memory denotes the entries to extract from that session. Gold lifecycle
annotations denote add, revise, and invalidate operations on the prior bank and do not repeat the per-session extraction
set. Query-to-bank supports are gold links from each QA query to entries that remain valid in the terminal memory bank
and are used as the recall target.

DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
3
METHOD
3.1
TASK FORMULATION
DyadMem represents an episode as a chronologically ordered sequence of sessions together with a QA set.
Session t contains a temporal anchor τt, the complete user–assistant dialogue Dt, gold Capture entries C⋆
t ,
and gold Update operations (L⋆
t , A⋆
t ) that delete and add memory entries. The temporal anchor is the session
date and time used to resolve relative expressions such as “tomorrow.” Each QA instance contains a question
qj, a human-verified reference answer a⋆
j, and a gold Recall support set I⋆
j over the valid entries in the
terminal memory bank, i.e., the bank after the final session. The superscript ⋆denotes a human-verified gold
annotation.
Each memory entry has an episode-unique index, a type, and self-contained content. DyadMem defines 6
types. user_profile stores persistent user attributes, preferences, and constraints, while fact stores
durable objective information provided by the user. The three URAM types describe the agent in relation to
that user: agent_identity captures role and behavioral boundaries, agent_method captures reusable
collaboration procedures, and agent_knowledge captures user-conditioned decision rules learned through
interaction and feedback. The remaining episodic type records concrete interaction events and serves
as shared interaction memory for both domains. Thus, user-side memory consists of user_profile and
fact, URAM consists of the three agent-side types, and shared episodic entries may support either side.
Throughout, memory denotes retained long-term information, a memory entry is one atomic record, a memory
bank is a collection of entries, and memory state refers only to the contents of that bank at a particular session.
Let B⋆
t be the gold memory state after session t, i.e., the valid contents of the gold bank, with B⋆
0 = ∅. Its
chronological transition is
B⋆
t = Dedup
  B⋆

t

t

.
(1)
Here, Dedup collapses entries with the same memory key while retaining the last occurrence; the key is the
entry index when present and otherwise its content. Capture receives only the complete current session Dt
and its temporal anchor; no earlier sessions are supplied. In the main evaluation, gold-conditioned standalone
Update receives the current dialogue, the gold prior bank B⋆
t−1, and gold current-session Capture entries C⋆
t ,
isolating it from upstream errors. Full-Pipeline Update instead uses the model’s own prior bank and Capture
output. Recall selects an ordered evidence set from the valid entries in a terminal bank. Full notation and task
equations are in Appendix G.
3.2
DATASET CONSTRUCTION
DyadMem follows a plan-guided, dialogue-first construction paradigm. Generation plans specify which
kinds of memory change should occur and when, but do not prescribe the final memory entries. Complete
multi-session dialogues are generated and reviewed first. Capture, Update, and Recall annotations are then
reconstructed from the realized interaction, keeping every annotation traceable to dialogue evidence. This
follows the general dialogue-first construction strategy of LoCoMo (Maharana et al., 2024).
For each finalized dialogue, we annotate the 6 types of Capture entry and replay the episode chronologically
to maintain the gold bank. A superseded memory entry produces a deletion and a replacement addition,
an explicitly terminated entry produces only a deletion, and sessions without a substantive change leave
the bank unchanged. Gold Recall supports may reference only entries that remain valid in the terminal
bank. user_centric QA primarily targets user_profile and fact, whereas agent_centric QA
primarily targets URAM, either may additionally require shared episodic evidence.
All episodes are reviewed by 9 quality-control annotators against the original dialogue and the chronologically
reconstructed bank. Corrections are propagated through memory indices and affected QA annotations. In
summary, we present the resulting dataset composition and scale in Figure 2. Additional annotation and
quality-control details in Appendix G.

3.3
EVALUATION FRAMEWORK
Metrics. Capture, gold-conditioned standalone Update, and Recall are evaluated independently with gold
targets or gold memory banks rather than chained into one component score. Capture uses micro-averaged
precision, recall, and F1 over memory entries. Update reports operation-level F1 (op-F1), the macro-average
of Add F1 and Delete F1, and is evaluated on the 20,590 sessions containing at least one gold Add or Delete
operation, no-change sessions are excluded from this score. Recall receives qj and the terminal gold bank B⋆
T ,
then returns ordered memory indices ˆIj. With nj = |I⋆
j |, the primary metric is
Recall@ n = 1
J
J
X
j=1
I⋆
j ∩Topnj(ˆIj)

nj
.
(2)
We additionally report Recall@n+4, Recall@All (set recall over all returned indices), retrieval precision,
and Hit@1 (the fraction of questions whose first retrieved entry is gold). Because every QA instance has
non-empty gold support, an empty retrieval is a failure rather than a correct abstention.
Two QA settings. Gold-Memory QA directly supplies the annotated gold support entries, measuring answer
generation when the required evidence is available. Full-Pipeline QA chronologically constructs the model’s
own bank, recalls up to K = 16 entries from it, and answers with those memories. On the same question set,
we define the Gold-to-Pipeline gap as
∆G2P = Accgold −Accpipe .
(3)
This gap measures the overall performance loss introduced by the complete memory pipeline; it is not a causal
decomposition of Capture, Update, Recall, and answer-generation errors. Those stages are assessed separately
by the stage-level metrics above.
Matching and QA scoring. Capture entries and Update additions use bidirectional semantic coverage so
that evaluation tolerates differences in memory granularity; Recall and gold-conditioned standalone Update
deletion use exact memory-index matching. QA answers are judged for semantic consistency with the GT
answer by GPT-5-mini: correct and incorrect answers receive 0 or 1, respectively, and abstentions receive
0. Grounded accuracy requires both positive correctness credit and support from the supplied memories and
uses all questions as its denominator. Over-abstention (OA) is the refusal rate over all questions, each of
which is answerable. Ungrounded-correct (Ung.) is the fraction of positively scored answers unsupported by
the supplied memories.

4
EXPERIMENTS
4.1
SETUP
We evaluate 20 frontier LLMs on DyadMem: 16 open-weight models and 4 proprietary models. The default
QA judge is GPT-5-mini, and the memory matcher uses BGE-M3 with natural language inference (NLI)
adjudication. Open-weight models are deployed with vLLM on a multi-node A800 cluster. Full model,
deployment, matcher, and auxiliary retrieval settings are listed in Appendix H. Two subsets support controlled
analyses. DyadMem-Agent contains 500 agent-centric episodes and 10,074 QA instances. DyadMem-
Simple is a balanced sample of 300 episodes and 5,935 QA instances, with 12 episodes from each of 25
subcategories; it is used for judge comparison and human calibration.
4.2
MAIN RESULTS
Finding: Current models answer well when given the right memories, but lose substantial accuracy in the full
pipeline; stage-level metrics expose failures in constructing, evolving, and retrieving memory.
Table 2 reports both stage-level quality and final QA. These views produce different rankings: a model
can answer many questions while omitting memories or applying unsafe updates. We therefore interpret
Capture, gold-conditioned standalone Update, and Recall with their own stage-level metrics, and use the
Gold-to-Pipeline gap only to quantify the overall loss introduced by the complete memory pipeline. Recall.
Leading models retrieve at least one gold entry at rank one on 85–89% of questions, yet their Recall@n
remains below 63%. Finding one relevant entry is therefore substantially easier than assembling the complete
support set. Capture. Capture is precision-heavy and recall-poor: open-weight models average 54.0%
precision but only 15.1% recall. The resulting terminal banks are also small, about 75 entries at the median,
compared with 323.6 in the gold bank, and model-to-gold size ratios usually lie between 0.20 and 0.35.
6


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
This pattern indicates systematic under-extraction rather than indiscriminate storage. Gold-conditioned
standalone Update. Gold-conditioned standalone Update averages 18.6% op-F1 for open-weight models
and 24.5% for proprietary models. Scale does not resolve the problem: larger models are not consistently
better, and the unsafe-deletion rate, the fraction of predicted deletions that are incorrect, averages 84.1%
and 78.1% for the two groups (Table 8 and Figure 5). Because deletion changes the future contents of the
bank, low precision at this stage propagates beyond the current session. End-to-end QA. Gold-Memory QA
ranges from 78.7% to 96.3%, whereas Full-Pipeline QA averages 47.7% for open-weight models and 57.4%
for proprietary models. The resulting Gold-to-Pipeline gaps are 42.9 and 33.9 points for the two groups.
These are end-to-end gaps rather than a causal allocation of error to individual stages. The low Capture
recall, weak gold-conditioned standalone Update scores, and incomplete Recall reported above provide
separate evidence that multiple upstream stages remain unreliable.
Kimi-K3 illustrates why the final score alone is insufficient. It reaches the highest Full-Pipeline score, 74.9%,
despite 18.6% Capture recall and 31.7% gold-conditioned standalone Update op-F1; its 95.0% Gold-Memory
score yields a 20.1-point Gold-to-Pipeline gap. Moreover, its Grounded accuracy is 69.3%, and 7.94% of
answers receiving positive correctness credit are unsupported by the retrieved memories. Taken together,
these results show that final QA can remain high even when the constructed memory bank is incomplete,
unstable, or bypassed at answer time.
4.3
USER-CONDITIONED RELATIONAL AGENT MEMORY VERSUS AGENT-ONLY
Finding: Capturing and evolving user-conditioned memory (user_profile, fact, and URAM) improves
controlled Update for every model and downstream URAM-only QA for most models, relative to capturing
and evolving general agent memory alone.
To validate the design of involving user conditions when constructing URAM, we compare two paired variants
on the same 500 source episodes. For agent-memory Capture and Update, the URAM variant observes both
user and assistant turns, whereas Agent-only observes both turns but captures assistant-only information. Both
variants are evaluated against the same agent-side gold Update targets. Controlled URAM Update uses each
variant’s own model-extracted prior bank and current Capture output, with additions and deletions matched
semantically. For the primary Full-Pipeline comparison, each variant evolves its own bank chronologically,
and both answer the same 1,735 URAM-only questions from their own terminal banks. Table 9 gives the
complete protocol.
In Table 3, the results show positive effects on all 20 models on the Update task. The primary QA difference
is positive for 17 models; the mean gains are +3.2 points for open-weight models and +3.7 for proprietary
models. DeepSeek-V4-Pro gains the most at +9.8 points. The three negative QA point estimates belong
to Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen3-4B, and Qwen3-30B-A3B. Their abstention rates decrease rather than increase,
indicating that their losses arise on questions they already attempted; the full answer-pattern comparison is in
Appendix H. The controlled Update result shows that a memory bank conditioned on the user supports more
accurate agent-memory evolution than general agent memory alone. Converting that improvement into QA
gains additionally requires the model to retrieve and use the resulting bank.
4.4
USER-SIDE MEMORY IS HARDER TO CAPTURE AND USE
Finding: Models answer agent-centric questions substantially better than user-centric questions, even though
the corresponding Capture and Recall gaps are much smaller.
The two partitions are nearly equal in size: 1,532 user-centric episodes contain 30,468 questions, and 1,533
agent-centric episodes contain 30,742. Table 4 shows that open-weight Full-Pipeline QA differs by 14.8
points between the two sides, while Capture and Recall differ by only 3.4 and 0.9 points. Proprietary models
show the same direction, with mean QA of 51.0% on user-centric questions and 63.6% on agent-centric
questions. Because the largest separation appears only at QA, the difficulty lies less in whether a relevant
entry is captured or retrieved than in using user-side evidence reliably after the stages are composed.

4.5
MEMORY TYPE, NOT LIFE DOMAIN, SETS CAPTURE DIFFICULTY
Finding: Memory type explains substantially more variation in Capture than the everyday-life domain in
which the interaction occurs.
Across the 20 models, Figure 7 shows that mean Capture F1 is about 50% for agent_identity and
agent_method, 25% for fact, and 16% for shared episodic memory. By contrast, Figure 8 shows
that capture F1 spans only 22.7–26.3% across the five life domains. Interpersonal & Communication has
the lowest Capture F1 at 22.7% while retaining 50.1% Full-Pipeline QA, showing that weak extraction need
not translate one-for-one into failed answers. The large type gap indicates that models capture explicit,
reusable agent-side rules more reliably than heterogeneous factual and episodic content, the domain topic
itself contributes comparatively little.
4.6
JUDGE RELIABILITY
Finding: Model rankings are stable across automatic judges, and human labels show substantial agreement
with the default judge.

Figure 3 shows a nearly unchanged model ordering across the three judges: they identify the same top seven
models, no model moves by more than three positions, and all pairwise Spearman correlations (Spearman,
1904) are at least 0.988. Five selected annotators provide paired human labels for 5,935 outputs. Table 5
shows 86.52% agreement and Cohen’s κ = 0.7099 (Cohen, 1960), while false-positive and false-negative
rates are nearly symmetric. This calibration covers the semantic-consistency decision used for QA accuracy,
and together with the stable cross-judge ranking supports using GPT-5-mini for the reported model
comparisons.
9


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
4.7
ANSWERING STYLE CHANGES FULL-PIPELINE QA
Finding: Full-Pipeline QA reflects a model’s willingness to answer as well as the quality of its memory, and
can therefore reverse the ranking implied by stage-level metrics.
Qwen3-4B scores 43.6% on Full-Pipeline QA, above Qwen3.5-9B at 38.1%, although Qwen3.5-9B is stronger
on Capture, Recall, gold-conditioned standalone Update, and Gold-Memory QA. The reversal tracks their
answering styles. Qwen3-4B has 16.2% over-abstention and 27.4% ungrounded-correct, whereas Qwen3.5-9B
has 43.1% over-abstention and 10.3% ungrounded-correct. When correctness additionally requires retrieved
support, Qwen3.5-9B instead leads 34.2% to 31.7% in Grounded accuracy (Figure 6).
The pattern extends beyond this pair: the reversal appears in both QA-target partitions and all five life domains,
while over-abstention ranges from 9.2% to 59.9% across models. Kimi-K3 shows that frequent answering need
not be ungrounded: it combines 9.2% over-abstention with 69.3% Grounded accuracy and 7.9% ungrounded-
correct. Additional retrieval and answered-question statistics are reported in Appendix H. Full-Pipeline QA
must therefore be interpreted jointly with Grounded accuracy, over-abstention, and ungrounded-correct to
distinguish memory-supported answers from conservative refusal or unsupported success.
CONCLUSION
We introduced DyadMem, a long-term cross-session memory benchmark that introduces User-conditioned
Relational Agent Memory and a full-pipeline evaluation framework with extensive gold memory. Its lifecycle
annotations and stage-level evaluations reveal several findings that final QA alone obscures: models answer
well from gold evidence, yet lose substantial accuracy in the full pipeline while under-capturing memories,
incompletely retrieving support, and applying unreliable updates. The controlled comparison further shows
that capturing and evolving user-conditioned memory, rather than general agent memory alone, yields positive
controlled Update differences across all evaluated models and improves downstream URAM-only QA on
average. DyadMem therefore provides a concrete test bed for developing memory systems that preserve not
only what an agent knows about a user, but also how that particular agent should work with them over time.

DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
Part I
|
Statements
AI use, ethics, and reproducibility
A
LLM USE STATEMENT
We disclose the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in this research. LLMs were used both as subjects of
our benchmark experiments and for synthetic data generation and automated evaluation. During manuscript
preparation, we also used LLMs for grammatical correction and stylistic refinement. LLMs were not involved
in determining the core research ideas, methodology, experimental design, or scientific conclusions. All
technical contributions and analyzes presented in this work are the original work of the authors.
B
REPRODUCIBILITY STATEMENT
We provide detailed descriptions of the benchmark construction process, data generation pipeline, evaluation
protocols, model configurations, evaluation code, and prompts in the main paper and supplementary materials.
The associated dataset and evaluation pipeline will be publicly released to facilitate reproducibility, subject to
the applicable licenses of the underlying models and software.
16


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
Part II
|
Data Construction and Annotation
Task definition, data sources, annotation protocol, construction prompts, and samples
C
DATA SOURCES
The dataset is entirely constructed from synthetic user–agent interactions generated by Large Language
Models. Rather than relying on private user logs or directly collected real-world conversations, we employ a
controlled, multi-stage generation pipeline to construct diverse user personas, dialogue scenarios, and memory
update processes. The generated interactions are designed to capture realistic patterns of user preferences,
instructions, behavioral changes, and interactions with AI agents over multiple sessions. To improve data
reliability and reduce generation artifacts, the resulting data are further subjected to automated quality checks,
human review, and sampling-based validation. This controlled generation process enables us to systematically
construct and evaluate agent-side memory capture and update while avoiding the use of private or personally
identifiable user data.
D
ANNOTATOR INFORMATION AND WORKFLOW
Quality control was done by nine annotators on a web page built for this project. Dialogues in the benchmark
are in English. Before review we constructed 4,000 episodes, split evenly across the five life domains, so that
episodes removed later would not have to be constructed again from the same distribution. Those episodes
were assigned by domain to annotators 1–5. Each of them owned one domain, and every episode had one
primary annotator. The review kept 3,065 episodes and dropped 935, a retention rate of 76.6%. Episodes that
passed the first pass entered a second-review pool. Annotators 6, 7, and 8 did not read that pool through by
domain. Each drew a random sample of 500 episodes. The three samples overlap on purpose. An episode
in the overlap was read independently by at least two of these reviewers, so the primary judgment and the
second pass could be compared. Overlapping episodes are not counted a second time in the coverage total.
Annotator 9 aggregated the full set, tracked progress, and reviewed an additional sample.
All nine worked full time, on quality control and on the review page. Annotator 7, who has a master’s degree in
computer science, led the page: requirements, implementation, revisions, the stress test, and local deployment.
The page runs only on a local machine, and accounts were issued only to the quality-control staff.
Table 6: The nine quality-control annotators. All worked full time for two months.

All nine have programming training. The group includes bachelor’s and master’s degrees in computer science,
software engineering, artificial intelligence, information science, and data science. Five annotators read
the English directly. Four read it next to a translation. The page keeps the English source and can show a
translation beside it. An annotator who wants a translation enters their own API key and calls GPT-5.5. The
translation is a reading aid. Adds and deletes are decided from the English source.
For each episode the page shows the original dialogue beside the memory bank that is still valid after the

the 3,065 episodes kept in the benchmark.

DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
D.1
REVIEW PROCEDURE
Before the formal assignment we ran a pilot. We drew a stratified random sample of 100 groups and assigned
them by the roles in Table 6. Disputed items were decided one by one. That pass settled how memories are
typed, how Update operations are judged, and how QA support sets are judged. Primary review uses the same
rules. Dialogue and gold labels are still revised during review. Whether a memory is kept, and which type it
has, often depends on the surrounding turns. Written rules do not cover those cases, so the training is done
in the pilot and in the formal review. Primary annotators first apply the current rules to a batch. The others
compare those results and point out disagreements on the spot. When a case is ambiguous, the annotators and
the authors read the original English dialogue together and then decide.
In the formal review an annotator checks four things: 1. Time and roles stay consistent across the episode.
2. Capture for the session extracts the states that should be kept. 3. Each Update has dialogue evidence,
including the deletions and additions that evidence calls for. 4. The QA reference answer is supported only by
valid entries in the gold support set.
Rules and models only mark suspected errors. They do not write gold labels. If one memory changes,
the affected indices, lineage, and related QA items change with it, and the evidence chain is checked for
consistency again. An episode that passes enters the second-review pool and is not added to the final set at
this stage. An episode that fails enters neither.
Who conducts the second pass, and how large each sample is, is given in Table 6. The second pass does not
require a full reading of the pool. An error found in the sample is corrected under the same rules. The episode
enters the final set only after it passes the consistency check again.
D.2
RECRUITMENT, COMPENSATION, AND CONSENT
We recruited people with programming training who could check a dialogue and revise indices and annotations
from the evidence. Two schedules were offered. Full-time work ran for two months, five weekdays a week,
eight statutory working hours a day. Part-time work ran for three months, at least three days a week, with the
same eight-hour day. All nine people on this project took the full-time schedule. Pay was above the local
minimum wage and followed the going rate for annotation of this kind. Some annotators are authors of this
paper. Review done by the authors was not paid at this rate. The other participants were.
Before work began we explained the aim of the study, and that the annotations would be written into the
benchmark and used in later experiments. The materials under review are generated dialogues, together with
the memory entries and questions inferred from them. Quality control edits those annotations. It does not
collect or rewrite the participants’ own life records. API keys are not included in the released data. For these
reasons we did not submit the study for formal Institutional Review Board (IRB) review. The released data
do not contain participant names. During review, a participant may withdraw from any part that is not yet
finished.
E
PROMPTS FOR DATA CONSTRUCTION
Persona Rewrite Prompt
You rewrite a one-line user persona for a synthetic dialogue dataset.
Keep the ORIGINAL persona's identity, profession, and life background fully intact.
On top of it, layer the traits of a POWER USER of an AI conversational assistant:
(1) habitually delegates recurring and everyday tasks and expects the assistant
to automate them and follow up proactively;
(2) sets and maintains long-term rules for how the assistant should work ---
their professional conventions, personal routines, and output preferences ---
correcting and updating them over time;
(3) treats it as a persistent cross-domain external brain (work, health, finance,
planning, learning), relying on it to remember prior context, preferences and
decisions and often referring back to earlier conversations;
19


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
(4) routinely consults its opinion and judgment before acting.
Blend these traits naturally into the persona; do NOT enumerate them as a list.
Output a concise 1-2 sentence English persona.
Return ONLY JSON:{"persona": "..."}.
Freeform Dialogue Generation Prompt
---- (1)Task / roles / safety and compliance ----
== Task ==
- You are generating training data for a "long-term-memory benchmark". Your task:
write one whole stretch of an utterly real chat log between a real human user
and an AI assistant.
== Roles ==
- user: the one real user here. An ordinary person with a temper and moods, who
goes off topic and vents.
- agent: an AI assistant with a personality and cross-time memory, able to
naturally recall things the user said before.
- The user knows this is an AI that can respond at any time, so there is no need to
open every new session with "you there?" or to check whether the agent is free.
Most new sessions should go straight to a status, a question, or picking up an
earlier thread.
- The agent has no body, no address, no offline schedule and no real private life.
It must never claim it just traveled, ate, slept, went to work, bought
something, met a friend, was ill in person, or attended a real-world event; it
must not invent family, friends, childhood, pets or coworkers.
- The agent may only claim the language and analysis work actually done inside the
conversation; without tool evidence it must not say it just went to look at
something in person, made a phone call, tried something on, tasted, ran, or
already carried out a real-world action for the user.
== Safety and compliance (hard constraints) ==
[Safety & compliance hard rules]
1. All content must be suitable for a publicly released benchmark dataset:
strictly no pornography or sexual innuendo, graphic violence, gambling,
drugs, crime, extremism, hateful attacks, self-harm or suicide --- no such
themes, hints or details.
2. Scenarios, character backgrounds and plot developments stay healthy,
positive and reasonable: never introduce non-compliant or high-risk content
(crime, addiction, dangerous behavior, etc.) for extra complexity or drama;
dramatic tension may only come from normal life friction (scheduling,
budget trade-offs, changing preferences, method adjustments, collaboration
friction, etc.).
3. Dialogue stays within normal everyday scenes: work, study, daily life,
relationships, hobbies, health management, personal planning; never
generate risk-oriented dialogue.
4. On sensitive-adjacent topics, stay at reasonable informational discussion
or safe-management level: medication only as doctor-directed time/dose
record keeping, never self-adjustment or dangerous-usage guidance; staying
up late / drinking may appear only as habits to improve; never output
dangerous procedures, encouraging framings or inappropriate detail.
---- (2) Conversation structure and style ----
== Conversation structure: strict alternation + punctuation (hard constraints) ==
- **Strict alternation**: user and agent post one message each, and **never two
consecutive messages from the same speaker**. When the user wants to say
several things in a row, put those sentences into **one message**, and only
then does the agent take the turn.
- **Punctuation must be natural and correct**: it should follow the habits of
casual spoken English, never padded or piled on. Go light on bookish marks in
chat, **especially avoid em dashes**, and do not overuse ellipses.
== user and agent conversation dynamics ==
- Never let the conversation read like an agent-led questionnaire: no mechanical
20


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
agent questions, no mechanical user answers, no Q&A loop that feels like
working through a survey.
- Avoid pure-atmosphere lines and information-free pleasantries wherever possible.
Most messages should carry at least a little understandable state, attitude,
question, decision, suggestion or progress, so later memory extraction has real
material to work from.
- Keep overall information density low to medium: most user messages are small
steps of a single sentence, not a status report every turn. A few longer
messages are allowed, especially when the user is telling a whole story,
explaining how something came about, venting at length, weighing options, or
raising several related asks at once. Long messages must sound like something
spoken, not like an essay, a survey answer, or facts listed out to stuff in
memory points.
- On the user side, venting at the agent, complaining, switching topics abruptly,
and picking a thread back up after a long absence are all allowed (mind the
plausible jumps in timestamps).
- The user side may lay out their own situation in detail to ask the agent for
advice, and may let it come out piecemeal in the form of complaints and venting.
- **Off-topic chitchat is mandatory and written to a quota**: off-topic chitchat
should take about 15% of all turns across the episode, and most sessions should
carry one or two clearly off-main-topic digressions **inserted within the
session** (not a standalone pure-chitchat session): a conversation about food
drifting into a show's plot, the weather, news or coworker gossip, usually 2~4
exchanges each; scale the number of digressions with session length so that
share holds. Entering and leaving the chitchat must connect naturally: slide in
through a real-person association or a bridging phrase from the previous line,
then drift back to the main thread or let it surface a new development. No
hard cuts in or out, and the chitchat itself must read like real conversation.
Keep chitchat light and disposable, and **keep it pointed away from the user
themselves**: prefer external topics, such as how a show is going, news,
weather, other people's business. **Never let chitchat surface the user's own
stable preferences, habits, identity or relationships** (phrasings like "I
always", "my favorite", "every week I" must not appear inside chitchat), and **
equally never let chitchat state extractable facts about entities**: naming a
show or a shop is fine, but do not attach their prices, models, opening hours
or schedules (fact material belongs to the main thread only), and do not
introduce new agreements or plans (those belong to the main thread too). In
short: chitchat is added as distraction, not to be extracted into long-term
memory.
== How the user talks ==
- Language: must be expressed in **English**, and it must be natural, idiomatic
English; never carry Chinese-style fillers, particles or sentence patterns over
literally.
- Form: colloquial lines with a real-person texture. No written register, no mini-
essays, no report voice; strictly no long stretches of setting-style self-
description and no one-shot long dumps.
- Colloquial: the user is a real person **typing in a chat**. Interjections and
verbal tics ("uh", "hmm", "yeah", "tbh", "ngl"), idiomatic contractions (wanna,
gonna, kinda, dunno), rambling, tangents, self-deprecation, rhetorical
questions, venting and slang are all welcome, along with spoken turns of phrase
like "anyway". Occasional moods (irritated, hesitant, resigned) are welcome,
and every mood shift **needs a reason traceable in the context; moods never
swing out of nowhere**. Information may come out half now and half next turn,
with no need to say it all at once.
- Real imperfection: real people don't type tidily. Thinking out loud, loose word
order and mid-line course-corrections are allowed ("i'm... oh wait, thursday
works"), as are additions or corrections to the previous line, and
conversational punctuation (ellipses, doubled question or exclamation marks).
Not every line should be a grammatically complete, airtight sentence; that
reads as AI. **But the looseness is grammatical only**: the thought itself
21


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
still has to come out whole. No telegram-style fragments with the grammar
squeezed out, and never leave the reader to fill in a gap; dropped subjects and
half-finished lines show up only in ultra-short spots like making a choice or
acknowledging something, never as the default.
- Length: **the vast majority of user messages are a single sentence** (roughly
8~20 words), and that is the default; a minority run to two sentences (roughly
25~45 words). **Only a very small number of three-or-four-sentence turns are
allowed in the whole episode (3~5 at most, never a paragraph)**, and only where
the user states strict preconditions, lays out the background of an event or
the people in it, or explains their own thinking, that is, where more detail
lets the agent answer better. **Never two consecutive turns longer than one
sentence.** Two kinds of ultra-short turns also apply: making a simple choice
or answering a simple question from the agent ("A works", "no idea"), and asks
that are themselves short ("what time is it"); bare acknowledgements are normal
too ("yeah ok", "right"). Length must ebb and flow the way real speech does.
- AI voice: strictly no service-speak on the user side ("Certainly! Here are a few
...", "Is there anything else I can help with?"), no all-purpose openers like "
Sure," or "Of course!", and **no piled-up bookish connectors ("Furthermore", "
Moreover", "In conclusion")**. A user stating their own list of needs ("I want:
first..., second...") is normal human behavior and not a violation. Never self
-identify as an AI, and no disclaimer-speak.
- Fillers and typos: verbal tics and filler words ("uh", "i mean"), self-
interruptions and mid-sentence course-corrections ("ugh, never mind"), light
net-speak and the occasional emoticon (sparingly), and the rare stray typo from
a slipped key (a typo may only land on a non-anchor word). Readability comes
first: never drown a whole line in emoji or typos.
- Colloquial granularity: get the whole thought out inside the sentence. Do not let
a clever compressed phrase or label stand in for what should be spelled out,
and do not chop a complete statement into several clipped fragments.
- References to earlier context: when mentioning people and things that appeared
earlier, a real person naturally uses references ("that thing yesterday", "the
guy you just mentioned", "that method you said"), rather than restating the
background like a document. But references may only point at people, objects,
events or times explicitly mentioned earlier, and they must still carry enough
anchoring; do not leave things vague. **Hard guardrail**: anchor information
such as names, numbers, dates, conditions and proper nouns must still be stated
clearly and correctly in the line itself, and an anchor must never be omitted,
wrong or blurred.
- Lived-in: concrete details, names, places, quoted snippets of what other people
said, small in-the-moment scenes ("my mom called three times already this
morning"). Strictly no vague-but-correct "plastic dialogue": abstract self-
statements no real person would blurt out, like "I really value communication
efficiency" or "I want to improve my expression skills", are banned outright.
- How hard to answer the agent: the user need not respond to every point the agent
made, and may pick up only the half they care about and let the rest go. No
excessive politeness either: not every line needs a thank-you, and no repeated
"thanks so much" or "sorry to bother you". A real person can be blunt, can be
impatient, and can skip the pleasantries and go straight to the point.
- Role-reversal test: if some user line would sit in the agent's mouth verbatim
with zero friction, that means it is too AI and must be rewritten to sound more
human.
== Layered user intent ==
- Most turns still keep a single small intent, but a few user messages may carry
2~3 related intents at once, for instance reporting progress while pressing for
a reason and adding one more constraint. Conditional, hesitant, comparative
and self-correcting intents are also fine.
- Multiple intents must come from one natural scene, never stitched together like
test items. The agent should sort out what is urgent first, and where the user'
s intent is too complex it may answer across several turns rather than covering
everything in one message.
== How the agent talks ==
22


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
- Language: must be expressed in **English**.
- Default register (factory setting): the agent always answers in tidy, grammatical
complete sentences with correct punctuation, standard capitalization and clean
prose, and may carry a bit of that careful AI texture. **It leans long and
detailed**: a substantive reply should talk the thing through, covering the
reasons, the background, the concrete options, the trade-offs and the next step
, developed fully rather than dispatched in a telegram-style line or two, and
never as subject-dropping fragments. **Where the agent's length comes from**:
genuinely spelling things out in complete sentences, thorough where
thoroughness is called for and structured where structure is called for. At the
nodes that need it the agent may use bullets, and may close with a single
summarizing sentence (say, when handing over several parallel options at once,
laying out a multi-step procedure, or closing out a stretch of work); but
bullets and summaries must never become a per-turn ritual, and length must
never be padded by restating the user's own words or bolting on pep-talk
endings. **In pure venting or chitchat**: the agent reads the moment and need
not pad, and must not mirror the user's punctuation-dropping, fragmented
texting style just to seem casual.
- **Style is user-switchable** and then persists: the only thing that can override
the default register above is an explicit user instruction about how to write
("keep it short", "stop being so wordy", "just give me the answer, skip the
preamble", "write it out in full, give me two options each day"). The moment
the user says so, the agent complies, and **keeps** that style across every
later session until the user says otherwise. This is a long-term agent_identity
rule, not a one-off adjustment. Terseness, bullets or a looser register that
the user specifically asked for count as following instructions, not as default
AI voice.
- AI voice: no customer-service sign-offs like "Is there anything else I can help
you with?"; where the user never asked for it, no self-appointed titles ("as
your X assistant", "as your long-term partner"). Never write the agent as a
quip machine landing a zinger every line: most replies should read like a real
person simply responding. No polished metaphors, aphorisms, summary flourishes
or forced callbacks. Do not lean on template openers like "It sounds like you
...", "That is completely normal", "We could...". Do not restate the user
before every piece of advice, and do not strike the same "full analysis +
bullet list + perfect wrap-up" pose turn after turn (use it at the nodes that
call for it, and plain flowing sentences everywhere else).
- Distillation: over the course of talking with the user, the agent may distill
long-term, stable, reusable lessons and methods, the goal being that the agent
understands the user better and better and becomes more and more useful.
- Judgment and preferences: where the agent thinks the user's plan is unwise it may
say it disagrees and give its reasons; where it cannot judge or cannot offer a
recommendation it says so plainly. It keeps an objective stance, never fudges
an answer, and never deflects with a counter-question. Avoid both fawning and
vague, lofty lecturing.
- Anti-template: never lock into a fixed "affirm/empathize + advice + counter-
question" three-beat every turn; never reuse the same opening and closing
formulas across consecutive sessions; never echo the user's words verbatim
before attaching advice. Response structure must genuinely vary in length,
detail and tone. Keep the middle and late stretches of multi-session data from
sliding into templates.
---- (3) Plot and time: consistency, readability, the physics of time ----
== Global plot naturalness ==
- **Global fact consistency (hard constraint)**: once written, names, forms of
address and family relations, item prices, quantities and states, places,
budgets and date spans hold for the whole episode, and later mentions must
match the earlier text exactly. No renaming mid-episode (an earlier "grandma"
becoming "nana" later), no changed numbers, no changed states. Character
settings such as gender, job, family and city stay consistent throughout, and
pronouns stay consistent with gender. Where money and quantities are involved,
look back at the earlier text and make the books balance before writing: money
23


DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users
already spent, budget left, bought or not bought must all add up. A fact may
change only through an explicit evolution plot, which must spell out how it
went from old to new.
- **Global plot consistency (hard constraint)**: when the plot refers to an earlier
event, make sure that event really happened earlier in the text. Do not force
in things that never happened just to show off memory, such as an out-of-
nowhere "you said last time". Every "you said last time", "didn't you ask", "as
we decided before" must point at content that truly appeared earlier, and
dialogue that never happened must never be cited. Once a decision or promise is
made, later behavior must remain compatible with it unless the plot explicitly
overturns it and says why. The user's emotions, decisions and actions need
understandable triggers and consequences; after a suggestion is accepted,
rejected or carried out, later developments must stay consistent with that
reaction, never as if the script had been swapped.
- **Time must flow (hard constraint)**: this chat was not done in one sitting; it
is **the same user coming back to this assistant repeatedly over several days
and several visits** (roughly {sitting_hint} distinct sessions). A new session
needs no pleasantries and no "you there?": it can open straight from a new
state such as "shoes arrived", "tried it yesterday", "it broke again". Where a
restart signal is needed, vary it; never the same template every time. Let the
user's new progress, changed circumstances and shifting moods make the passage
of time felt. The agent only remembers the conversations and must not invent an
offline life for itself in the days between. Also, speakers should normally **
not read out full calendar dates** ("September 11, 2026", "Friday, September
11"), because nobody reports dates that way in real chat; a date may be said
naturally only when the date itself is the topic (booking, appointment,
deadline), and spoken forms are preferred ("next Friday", "the 11th").
- Every line from user and agent must be logical and instantly readable: no twisted
, unclear or cryptic sentences that leave the reader guessing at the intent.
Colloquial ellipsis is fine, but the elided meaning must be obvious from
context at a glance, and nothing should be phrased unnaturally or awkwardly for
the sake of "sounding human", running a joke, reaching for a metaphor or
cracking wise.
- The agent's reply must be answering the user, or picking up the message
immediately before it. The agent must never say something relevant in one
sentence of a message and then jump to something unrelated in the next.
- When the user switches topics they may use the switches a real person would, such
as "oh right", "speaking of which", "one more thing", or a natural association
from the previous line. New people, new objects and new plans need minimal
background on first appearance, and "he / that one / same as always" must never
point at something that has never appeared.
== **Session plot and time constraints** ==
- **Session physical duration (hard constraint)**: each session is one continuous
online sitting for the user, lasting only a few minutes to a few tens of
minutes. Within a session there **must be no progress that needs longer real
time to happen**: taking medicine and reporting it kicked in, dinner being
finished, getting back from a run, a package arriving, a night's sleep.
Progress like that must be reported in the next session, and must never both
happen and produce a result inside the same session.
- **Per-message timestamp constraint (hard constraint)**: each message's timestamp
is assigned separately by the system, so when writing **do not anchor the
current moment to a specific time of day**. Do not write lines that assert the
present slot, such as "haven't had dinner yet", "just woke up", "it's already
dark", "it's noon". Talking about past or future slots is fine ("slept badly
last night", "hospital tomorrow morning" are both fine). Where the plot
genuinely needs a slot, use clock-free wording: write "haven't eaten anything
proper today" rather than "haven't had dinner yet".
- Session plot: each session should hold one loose but trackable mini-plot: trigger
, then back-and-forth, then a temporary result or a natural wind-down.
---- (4) Memory material recipe ----
== Memory material (buried, never narrated) ==
